**The Sira gives the occasion.** The Garden is named where men were asked to give something: protection and war at 'Aqaba, a house left in Mecca, wealth, a life in battle. When the Medinans asked "So what is ours?", the answer was "The Garden" (2.13). As in the Sahihs, the Sira denies the Garden to the fighter whose motive was other than God's.

**Fear and hope.** The claim tested in 2.14 holds in its first two parts, with qualifications. It holds in its third part only in part:
- death in jihad carries the most prominent guarantee;
- the guarantee is to a class, bound to intention and to debt;
- other deeds carry the same wording;
- no individual may be declared to be in the Garden.

**Islam varies, and the variation runs along these lines:**
- **The ground.** Is it deed, or mercy?
- **The scope.** Does salvation reach the community, the one sect, or those who hold the wilaya?
- **The authority.** Under whom is the jihad that the Garden rewards fought: any amir, or the Imam alone?

---

## 3. Buddhism

### Sources and conventions

**The Pali Canon.**
- The Pali is the Mahāsaṅgīti root text, and the English is Bhikkhu Sujato's (public domain, CC0).
- Both come from SuttaCentral's `bilara-data`, at commit `b8defc2`.
- The segment IDs (for example `sn38.1:2.3`) allow each line to be checked against the data.

**Nineteenth-century translations,** close to the book's register:
- Max Müller's *Dhammapada* (Sacred Books of the East X, 1881) is quoted where it reads well.
- Fausbøll's *Sutta-Nipâta* (SBE X, 1881) is quoted for the Upasīva verses.
- Müller's *Sukhâvatî-vyûha* (SBE XLIX, 1894) and Kern's *Lotus* (SBE XXI, 1884) cover the Mahayana.
- All are public domain, from the archive.org scans.

A short gloss is added wherever Sujato's English departs from the literal Pali.

### 3.1 The end state, named from the text

The final end is **nibbāna** (Sanskrit *nirvāṇa*). The word means "going out, being quenched", as a fire goes out, and Sujato renders it "extinguishment". The texts also call it:

| Pali | Literal sense | Where |
|---|---|---|
| *amata* | the deathless | SN 43.25; Dhp 21; MN 26 ("doors to freedom from death") |
| *asaṅkhata* | the unconditioned, "not put together" | SN 43.12; Ud 8.3; Iti 44 |
| *ajāta, abhūta, akata* | the unborn, unbecome, unmade | Ud 8.3 |
| *pāra* | the far shore | SN 43.16; Dhp 85 to 86; MN 22 |
| *khema*, *yogakkhema anuttara* | safety; the unsurpassed safety from the yoke | SN 43.28; Dhp 23; MN 26 |
| *santi*, *santa pada* | peace; the peaceful state | Dhp 202, 368; SN 43.24 |
| *dukkhassa anta* | the end of suffering | Ud 8.1, 8.4 |
| *taṇhakkhaya* | the wasting away of craving | SN 43.29; Dhp 354 |
| *akuppā vimutti* | unshakeable release | SN 56.11 |
| *dīpa, leṇa, tāṇa, saraṇa, parāyana* | island, rock shelter, protection, refuge, final resort | SN 43.40 to 43.44 |
| *sadattha* | "one's own good", which the arahant "has reached" (*anuppattasadattho*) | Iti 44; SN 51.15; Dhp 166 |

The Asaṅkhata Saṃyutta (SN 43.12 to 43.44) gives thirty-three names in thirty-three short discourses. Each says "I will teach you X and the path that leads to X." Each defines X as "the ending of greed, hate, and delusion." The names, with Sujato's English:

the unconditioned (*asaṅkhata*), the uninclined (*anata*; read as *anta*, "the end", in the Burmese text), the undefiled (*anāsava*), the truth (*sacca*), the far shore (*pāra*), the subtle (*nipuṇa*), the very hard to see (*sududdasa*), the undecrepit (*ajajjara*), the everlasting (*dhuva*), the not worn out (*apalokita*), the invisible (*anidassana*), the unproliferated (*nippapañca*), the peaceful (*santa*), freedom from death (*amata*), the sublime (*paṇīta*), the state of grace (*siva*), the sanctuary (*khema*), the ending of craving (*taṇhākkhaya*), the incredible (*acchariya*), the amazing (*abbhuta*), the untroubled (*anītika*), the not liable to trouble (*anītikadhamma*), extinguishment (*nibbāna*), the unafflicted (*abyābajjha*), dispassion (*virāga*), purity (*suddhi*), release (*mutti*), not clinging (*anālaya*), the island (*dīpa*), the asylum (*leṇa*), the shelter (*tāṇa*), the refuge (*saraṇa*), the final haven (*parāyana*).

The chapter's summary verse closes the list: *Nibbānametaṁ sugatena desitaṁ*, "this is nibbāna, as taught by the Sugata" (`sn43.44:3.4`).

### 3.2 What nibbāna is said to be

- **An ending.**
  - *Yo kho, āvuso, rāgakkhayo dosakkhayo mohakkhayo — idaṁ vuccati nibbānan'ti.*
  - "Reverend, the ending of greed, hate, and delusion is called extinguishment." (SN 38.1, `sn38.1:2.3–2.4`)
- **Unborn and unmade.**
  - *Atthi, bhikkhave, ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ.*
  - Literally: "There is, monks, an unborn, unbecome, unmade, unconditioned."
  - Sujato: "There is, mendicants, freedom from rebirth, freedom from what has been produced, made, and conditioned. If there were no freedom from rebirth ... then you would find no escape here from rebirth." (Ud 8.3, `ud8.3:3.1–3.2`)
- **No place.** "There is, mendicants, that dimension where there is no earth, no water, no fire, no wind ... no this world, no other world, no moon or sun. There, mendicants, I say there is no coming or going or remaining or passing away or reappearing ... Just this is the end of suffering." (Ud 8.1)
- **Two stages.** Iti 44 distinguishes:
  - "the element of extinguishment with residue": the arahant still living, whose "five sense faculties still remain";
  - "the element of extinguishment with no residue": for him, "everything that's felt, being no longer relished, will become cool right here."
  - *Upādi* means fuel, that which is taken up; *sīti bhavissanti* means "will become cool."
- **The highest happiness.**
  - *Nibbānaṁ paramaṁ sukhaṁ.* (Dhp 203, 204)
  - Müller: "Hunger is the worst of diseases, the body the greatest of pains; if one knows this truly, that is Nirvâna, the highest happiness." (Dhp 203)
  - Müller: "Health is the greatest of gifts, contentedness the best riches; trust is the best of relationships, Nirvâna the highest happiness." (Dhp 204)
- **A flame gone out.**
  - Fausbøll: "As a flame blown about by the violence of the wind ... goes out, cannot be reckoned (as existing), even so a Muni, delivered from name and body, disappears, and cannot be reckoned (as existing)." (Sutta Nipāta 5.7, v. 1074)
  - Sujato, next verse but one: "One who has disappeared cannot be defined ... When all things have been eradicated, eradicated, too, are all ways of speech." (v. 1076)
- **A fire that went out, and a man like the ocean.** In MN 72, Vaccha is asked in which direction a fire went when it went out. He answers that the question "doesn't apply ... When that runs out, and no more fuel is added, the fire is reckoned to have become quenched due to lack of fuel." The Buddha applies this to the freed man: "A realized one is freed from reckoning in terms of form. They're deep, immeasurable, and hard to fathom, like the ocean. 'They're reborn', 'they're not reborn', 'they're both reborn and not reborn', 'they're neither reborn nor not reborn'—none of these apply."
- **The arahant's own report.**
  - *Khīṇā jāti, vusitaṁ brahmacariyaṁ, kataṁ karaṇīyaṁ, nāparaṁ itthattāya.*
  - Literally: "Birth is exhausted, the holy life lived, what was to be done is done, nothing further for this state."
  - Sujato: "Rebirth is ended, the spiritual journey has been completed, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this place." (DN 2, `dn2:97.6`)
  - The Buddha's own declaration at the first sermon: "My freedom is unshakable; this is my last rebirth; now there'll be no more future lives." (SN 56.11)

The final end, then, is not a place of reward to which a self goes. It is the ending of the craving that, in the text, produces the next life.

### 3.3 Intermediate ends

**The four stages.** MN 6 gives them as things "a mendicant might wish" for:
- **Stream-enterer** (*sotāpanna*): "with the ending of three fetters, become a stream-enterer, not liable to be reborn in the underworld, assured, destined for awakening."
- **Once-returner** (*sakadāgāmī*): "with ... the weakening of greed, hate, and delusion, become a once-returner, coming back to this world once only, then making an end of suffering."
- **Non-returner** (*anāgāmī*): "with the ending of the five lower fetters, be reborn spontaneously and become extinguished there, not liable to return from that world."
- **Arahant:** "realize the undefiled freedom of heart and freedom by wisdom ... in this very life."

Further texts on the stages:
- **The ten fetters** (AN 10.13):
  - The five lower are "substantialist view, doubt, misapprehension of precepts and observances, sensual desire, and ill will."
  - The five higher are "desire for rebirth in the realm of luminous form, desire for rebirth in the formless realm, conceit, restlessness, and ignorance."
- **The stream-enterer has "at most seven rebirths":** "They will transmigrate at most seven times among gods and humans and then make an end of suffering." (AN 3.87)
- **The stages tied to the three trainings** (AN 3.87):
  - ethics fulfilled gives stream-entry or once-return;
  - ethics and immersion fulfilled give non-return;
  - all three fulfilled give arahantship.
  - "If you practice partially you succeed partially. If you practice fully you succeed fully."
- **The Pure Abodes** (*suddhāvāsa*): AN 4.124 gives rebirth among "the gods of the pure abodes" to the meditator who sees his absorptions as impermanent. The passages fetched do not pair the Pure Abodes with the non-returner in one sentence.

**Heaven** (*sagga*, the deva worlds) is a lesser end, and it does not last.
- **What is promised.** In AN 8.36, the gods of each heaven "surpass" the others "in ten respects: heavenly lifespan, beauty, happiness, glory, sovereignty, sights, sounds, smells, tastes, and touches." The grade of heaven rises with the amount of giving and ethics.
- **AN 5.34, in verse:** "Having given food in abundance to those worthy of a religious donation, after passing from the human realm, the givers go to heaven. And when they have arrived there in heaven, they enjoy all the pleasures they desire."
- **It ends.** In AN 4.123, the meditator reborn in a Brahmā world stays "until the lifespan of those gods is spent." He then goes "to hell or the animal realm or the ghost realm," unless he is the Buddha's disciple: "a disciple of the Buddha stays there until the lifespan of those gods is spent, then they're extinguished in that very life."
- **It is worth less than the path.** In SN 55.1 a world-ruling king is reborn among the gods of the Thirty-Three, "escorted by a band of nymphs," and is "not exempt from hell." A disciple "wears rags and feeds on scraps of almsfood" but has four qualities: confidence in the Buddha, the teaching and the Saṅgha, and unbroken ethics. The text concludes: "gaining these four continents is not worth a sixteenth part of gaining these four things."

### 3.4 Conditions

**The Four Noble Truths** (SN 56.11, the first sermon):
1. **Suffering:** "Rebirth is suffering; old age is suffering; illness is suffering; death is suffering ... In brief, the five grasping aggregates are suffering."
2. **Its origin is craving** (*taṇhā*, literally "thirst"): "It's the craving that leads to future lives ... craving for sensual pleasures, craving for continued existence, and craving for nonexistence" (*kāmataṇhā, bhavataṇhā, vibhavataṇhā*).
3. **Its cessation:** "the fading away and cessation of that very same craving ... giving it away, letting it go, releasing it, and not clinging to it" (*yo tassāyeva taṇhāya asesavirāganirodho cāgo paṭinissaggo mutti anālayo*).
4. **The path:** "this noble eightfold path, that is: right view, right purpose, right speech, right action, right livelihood, right effort, right mindfulness, and right immersion."

**The path factors defined** (SN 45.8):
- Right speech is "refraining from speech that's false, backbiting, harsh, or nonsensical."
- Right action is "refraining from killing living creatures, stealing, and unchastity."
- Right livelihood is "when a noble disciple gives up wrong livelihood and earns a living by right livelihood."
- Right effort is to strive "so that bad, unskillful qualities don't arise ... are given up," and so that "skillful qualities arise ... increase, mature, and are fully developed."
- Right mindfulness and right immersion are the meditative training: the four establishments of mindfulness and the four absorptions.

**The three trainings** (AN 3.89): "the higher ethics, the higher mind, and the higher wisdom" (*sīla, citta* or *samādhi, paññā*).

**Entry to the stream:**
- **The four factors** (SN 55.5): "associating with true persons, listening to the true teaching, rational application of mind, and practicing in line with the teaching."
- **"The stream is simply this noble eightfold path"** (SN 55.5).
- **The four qualities of the stream-enterer** (SN 55.1): "experiential confidence" (*aveccappasāda*, confidence settled in its object) in the Buddha, the teaching and the Saṅgha, and ethics "loved by the noble ones, unbroken."

**The conditions for heaven, which are open to laymen:**
- **The ten wholesome courses of action** (MN 41, Sāleyyaka): "Principled and moral conduct is the reason why some sentient beings, when their body breaks up, after death, are reborn in a good place, a heavenly realm." The ten:
  - **By body:** giving up killing, stealing and sexual misconduct.
  - **By speech:** giving up lying, backbiting, harsh speech and nonsense.
  - **By mind:** not coveting ("Oh, if only their belongings were mine!"); a kind heart; and right view ("There are fruits and results of good and bad deeds. There is an afterlife").
- **The three grounds of merit** (AN 8.36):
  - *Dānamayaṁ puññakiriyavatthu, sīlamayaṁ puññakiriyavatthu, bhāvanāmayaṁ puññakiriyavatthu.*
  - "Giving, ethical conduct, and meditation are all grounds for making merit."
- **Giving** (AN 5.34, Sīha): the giver "is dear and beloved to many people. This is a fruit of giving that's apparent in the present life. Furthermore, when a giver's body breaks up, after death, they're reborn in a good place, a heavenly realm. This is a fruit of giving to do with lives to come."
- **The lay duties** (DN 31): the householder who keeps them is "practicing to win in both worlds, and they succeed in this world and the next. When their body breaks up, after death, they're reborn in a good place, a heavenly realm."

**The graduated talk** (*anupubbikathā*). The texts give the order in which the Buddha teaches a layman, and the order is the redirection itself:
1. giving;
2. ethics;
3. heaven;
4. the drawbacks of sensual pleasure;
5. the benefit of renunciation;
6. only then, the Four Truths.

The text of AN 8.12:
- *Atha kho bhagavā sīhassa senāpatissa anupubbiṁ kathaṁ kathesi, seyyathidaṁ — dānakathaṁ sīlakathaṁ saggakathaṁ, kāmānaṁ ādīnavaṁ okāraṁ saṅkilesaṁ nekkhamme ānisaṁsaṁ pakāsesi.*
- "Then the Buddha taught Sīha step by step, with a talk on giving, ethical conduct, and heaven. He revealed the drawbacks of sensual pleasures, so sordid and corrupt, and the benefit of renunciation. And when the Buddha knew that Sīha's mind was ready, pliable, rid of hindrances, elated, and confident he revealed the special teaching of the Buddhas: suffering, its origin, its cessation, and the path." (`an8.12:28.1–28.3`)

The same formula occurs at DN 3, DN 5, MN 56, and in the Vinaya, where it is given to Yasa, a rich merchant's son (Mahāvagga I.7).

### 3.5 How the text redirects self-interest

The Pali texts appeal openly to the hearer's own good. They then use the desire for the goal to end desire itself.

- **Craving used to end craving.**
  - *Taṇhāsambhūto ayaṁ, bhagini, kāyo taṇhaṁ nissāya. Taṇhā pahātabbā.*
  - "This body is produced by craving. Relying on craving, you should give up craving." (AN 4.159, `an4.159:4.3–4.4`)

  The craving that is relied on is craving for the goal. A monk hears that another has reached release and thinks, "Oh, when will I too realize the undefiled freedom of heart." Then: "After some time, relying on craving, they give up craving" (*taṇhaṁ nissāya taṇhaṁ pajahati*, `an4.159:6.8`). Conceit is used the same way: "Well, that venerable can realize ... Why can't I?" (`an4.159:7.7–7.9`).
- **The desire that ends on arrival.** A brahmin objects: "it's not possible to give up desire by means of desire." Ānanda asks whether he ever wished to walk to the park and found that "when you arrived at the park, the corresponding desire faded away." The arahant, he says, "formerly had the desire to attain perfection, but when they attained perfection the corresponding desire faded away" (SN 51.15). *Chanda*, desire, is the same word for the desire given up and the desire to reach the goal.
- **The lesser happiness for the greater.**
  - *Mattāsukhapariccāgā, passe ce vipulaṁ sukhaṁ; caje mattāsukhaṁ dhīro, sampassaṁ vipulaṁ sukhaṁ.*
  - Müller: "If by leaving a small pleasure one sees a great pleasure, let a wise man leave the small pleasure, and look to the great." (Dhp 290)
- **One's own good first.**
  - *Attadatthaṁ paratthena, bahunāpi na hāpaye; attadatthamabhiññāya, sadatthapasuto siyā.*
  - Literally: "One's own good, for another's however great, one should not neglect; knowing one's own good, one should be intent on one's own good."
  - Müller: "Let no one forget his own duty for the sake of another's, however great" (Dhp 166). He notes that *attha* "must here be taken in a moral sense, as 'duty' rather than as 'advantage.'"
  - AN 4.95 ranks "the individual who practices to benefit themselves, but not others" above the one who benefits others but not himself. It ranks highest the one who benefits both. AN 4.96 defines that benefit as the removal of one's own "greed, hate, and delusion," and encouraging others to remove theirs.
- **The self as its own lord.**
  - *Attā hi attano nātho, ko hi nātho paro siyā.*
  - Müller: "Self is the lord of self, who else could be the lord? With self well subdued, a man finds a lord such as few can find." (Dhp 160)
- **Self-love, rightly understood.**
  - In SN 3.4 those who do bad "treat themselves like an enemy," and those who do good "treat themselves like a loved one."
  - The verse: "That's why you should do good, investing in the future life. The good deeds of sentient beings support them in the next world." The Pali for "investing in the future life" is *nicayaṁ samparāyikaṁ*, literally "an accumulation for the hereafter."
  - SN 3.8: "Having explored every quarter with the mind, one finds no one dearer than oneself. Likewise for others, each holds themselves dear." The Pali ends *tasmā na hiṁse paramattakāmo*: literally "therefore one who loves himself should not harm another." Sujato's "one who desires self-knowledge" departs from the literal sense.
- **One's own wish as the measure for others** (SN 55.7). Householders ask for sandalwood, garlands, gold, and heaven after death. The Buddha begins from "an explanation of the Dhamma that is relevant for oneself" (*attupanāyika*): "I want to live and don't want to die; I want to be happy and recoil from pain ... The thing that is disliked by me is also disliked by others. Since I dislike this thing, how can I inflict it on someone else?" See also Dhp 130 to 132.
- **Treasure that thieves cannot take** (Khuddakapāṭha 8, Nidhikaṇḍa). The poem sets buried savings, which "will fail to aid them all the time," against merit:
  - "But by giving and morality, restraint and self-control, a woman or man keeps their savings safe."
  - "You don't have to share it with others, no thief makes off with your savings" (*acorāharaṇo nidhi*).
  - "Such savings grant every desire of gods and humans too ... Human success, heavenly delight, attaining extinguishment: through this they have it all."
- **Merit as happiness, and as a field.**
  - *Sukhassetaṁ, bhikkhave, adhivacanaṁ ... yadidaṁ puññāni.* "'Good deeds' is a term for happiness, for what is likable, desirable, and agreeable." (Iti 22)
  - The Saṅgha is "the supreme field of merit for the world" (*anuttaraṁ puññakkhettaṁ lokassa*): gifts are seed, and the community is the field.
  - MN 142 grades the "returns" on gifts by the recipient. A gift to an animal yields "a hundred times." A gift to "someone practicing to realize the fruit of stream-entry may be expected to yield incalculable, immeasurable returns."
  - Iti 26: "if sentient beings only knew, as I do, the fruit of giving and sharing, they would not eat without first giving."
- **The reward promised, then not needed** (Udāna 3.2). Nanda longs for the woman he left. The Buddha shows him heavenly nymphs and says, "I guarantee you five hundred dove-footed nymphs." The monks call Nanda "a hireling ... a lackey: he leads the spiritual life for the sake of nymphs." Shamed, he practises and becomes an arahant. He releases the Buddha from the promise, and the Buddha answers: "As soon as your mind was freed from defilements by not grasping, I was released from that promise."
- **The teaching itself let go** (MN 22). "I have taught a simile of the teaching as a raft: for crossing over, not for holding on ... By understanding the simile of the raft, you will even give up the teachings, let alone what is not the teachings."
- **Household prudence, for the layman** (DN 31). "They pick up riches as bees roaming round pick up pollen ... One portion is to enjoy. Two parts invest in work. And the fourth should be kept for times of trouble."

The pattern:

1. The layman's self-interest is first given heaven, a lasting store of merit, and a good name, all gained by giving and ethics.
2. The monk's self-interest is then given release.
3. Release is defined as the end of the self-interest that sought it: "relying on craving, they give up craving."

The Christian end keeps the person and perfects him (1 John 3:2). The Pali end leaves nothing of which "they're reborn" or "they're not reborn" can be said (MN 72).

### 3.6 Mahayana, briefly: the Pure Land and the Bodhisattva

**The Smaller Sukhāvatīvyūha.**
- **What the land is.**
  - It lies in the West, "a world called Sukhâvatî (the happy country)."
  - It has "lotus lakes, adorned with the seven gems," and birds that "perform a concert" proclaiming the virtues and powers.
  - It has no hells.
  - Its Buddha is called Amitâyus because "the length of life (âyus) ... of that Tathâgata and of those men there is immeasurable (amita)."
  - (Müller, SBE XLIX, 1894, §§2 to 8, pp. 91 to 97)
- **Not a wage.** "Beings are not born in that Buddha country of the Tathâgata Amitâyus as a reward and result of good works performed in this present life." (§10, p. 98). Kumārajīva's Chinese (T366) has instead: "One cannot, by few roots of good, merit, and causal conditions, attain birth in that land."
- **The condition.** "Whatever son or daughter of a family shall hear the name of the blessed Amitâyus, the Tathâgata, and having heard it, shall keep it in mind, and with thoughts undisturbed shall keep it in mind for one, two, three, four, five, six or seven nights,—when that son or daughter of a family comes to die, then that Amitâyus ... will stand before them at their hour of death ... After their death they will be born in the world Sukhâvatî." (§10, p. 99). The Chinese counts days where Müller's Sanskrit has nights.

**The Larger Sukhāvatīvyūha.**
- **The vow.** Müller's Sanskrit vow 19 is the "eighteenth vow" of the Chinese tradition. Bunyiu Nanjio's note in the same volume (p. 73) gives this numbering.
  - **Müller:** "if those beings who in immeasurable and innumerable Buddha countries, after they have heard my name ... should direct their thought to be born in that Buddha country of mine ... if these should not be born in that Buddha country, even those who have only ten times repeated the thought (of that Buddha country), barring always those beings who have committed the (five) Ânantarya sins, and who have caused an obstruction and abuse of the good Law, then may I not obtain the highest perfect knowledge." (p. 15)
  - **The Chinese T360** reads 至心信樂欲生我國，乃至十念. Literally: "with sincere mind, believing and joyful, wishing to be born in my land, even ten thoughts."
- **What the inhabitants have.**
  - "All enjoyments and pleasures, exactly like the Paranirmitavasavartin gods" (the highest of the Pali sense-sphere heavens).
  - Food perceived without being eaten.
  - Palaces that appear as desired, "surrounded by seven times seven thousands of Apsarases." (§19, pp. 40 to 42)
  - Yet vow 10: "if in that Buddha country of mine the beings who are born there should form any idea of property, even with regard to their own body, then may I not obtain the highest perfect knowledge." (p. 13)
  - Vow 11: all are to be "firmly established ... till they have reached Mahâparinirvâna." The Pure Land is a station on the way to nirvana, not the end.

**The Lotus Sutra** (Kern, SBE XXI, 1884). The end is Buddhahood for all: "There is but one vehicle, Sâriputra, and that the Buddha-vehicle" (p. 43). The burning-house parable states the method outright.
- **The lure.** A father cannot get his children out of a burning house. Knowing "the disposition of the boys," he promises them the toys they want: "bullock-carts, goat-carts, deer-carts ... Come, run out, leave the house; to each of you I shall give what he wants." They rush out, "pushing each other on with the cry of 'Who shall arrive first, the very first?'" (p. 74)
- **The gift.** Once they are out, he gives each of them something better than they asked for: "bullock-carts only, made of seven precious substances" (p. 75).
- **The two motives.** The disciples fly the world "for the sake of their own complete Nirvâna." The Bodhisattva works "for the sake of the common weal and happiness, out of compassion to the world ... for the sake of the complete Nirvâna of all beings" (p. 80).

---

## 4. Side by side

| | Christianity (New Testament) | Islam (Qur'an) | Buddhism (Pali Canon) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Name of the end** | eternal life; the kingdom of heaven, the kingdom of God; paradise; the holy city, new Jerusalem | al-Janna, the Garden; al-Firdaws; the Gardens of Eden and of Bliss; the abode of peace; God's good pleasure (*ridwan*) | nibbāna, "extinguishment"; the deathless; the unconditioned; the far shore |
| **What it is said to be** | God dwelling with men; no death, sorrow or pain; a raised, incorruptible body; seeing God "face to face"; no marriage | rivers, fruit and shade; silk and gold; purified spouses and *hur 'in*; no idle talk; whatever souls desire; God's pleasure, "greater" | "the ending of greed, hate, and delusion"; no further birth; the freed man "cannot be reckoned" |
| **Does the person remain** | yes, raised and made like Christ (1 John 3:2; Phil 3:21) | yes, in bodily delight and in God's pleasure | the text will not say he is "reborn" or "not reborn" (MN 72) |
| **The condition** | one: faith in Christ, with repentance | many. In the Qur'an: belief with righteous deeds; fear of God; obedience; patience; repentance; spending and feeding the poor; prayer and alms; chastity; keeping trusts; emigration; striving; fighting in the way of God. The Hadith add the testimony and many particular acts. Fighting is the largest category in the Sahihs. In the Twelver texts the wilaya of the Imams governs every deed | the Eightfold Path and the ending of craving. For heaven: giving, ethics, and the ten wholesome courses |
| **Gift or wage** | entry a gift, "not of works"; reward by labour within it (1 Cor 3:8 to 15) | both grounds stated together: "for what you used to do" and "the bounty of God" (7:43, 57:21). The Sahihs: "His deed will never admit anyone to the Garden ... unless God covers me with bounty and mercy" (Bukhari 5673) | reached by one's own practice ("if you practice fully you succeed fully"). The Pure Land is "not ... as a reward and result of good works" |
| **The language of exchange** | treasure laid up; profit and loss of the soul; selling all to buy the field | a sale (9:111), a trade (61:10), a loan (2:245), a wage (3:136), the Day of Mutual Loss (64:9). In the Hadith: God's guarantee (Muslim 1876); "the merchandise of God is the Garden" (Tirmidhi 2450) | savings no thief can take (Khp 8); returns on gifts (MN 142); the field of merit |
| **How self-interest is redirected** | treasure moved to heaven; repayment moved from men to the Father; ambition turned to service; theft turned to labour, and labour to giving | this world "little" beside the hereafter; the self and wealth sold for the Garden | the lesser happiness given up for the greater; merit as a store; craving used to end craving |
| **Conduct toward others** | love of enemies; feasts for those who cannot repay; service; honest labour "that he may have to give" | feeding the poor, the orphan and the captive; freeing slaves; keeping trusts; restraining anger and pardoning; fighting "in the way of God"; refusing affection to kin who oppose God | not harming others, since "each holds themselves dear"; giving; truthful and reconciling speech |
| **Degrees** | "one star differeth from another star in glory"; ten cities and five | strivers above those who sit; the foremost, the companions of the right, and the companions of the left; two pairs of Gardens | four stages; heavens graded by merit; "if you practice partially you succeed partially" |

## 5. Observations for the later chapter

These are the author's points as the texts bear them out. They are working notes, not chapter prose.

1. **None of the three tells the hearer to stop seeking his own good.**
   - Christianity: "Lay up for yourselves treasures in heaven" (Matt 6:20).
   - Islam: "shall I direct you to a trade that will save you" (61:10).
   - Buddhism: "If by leaving a small pleasure one sees a great pleasure, let a wise man leave the small pleasure, and look to the great" (Dhp 290).

   Each takes the self-interest that is there and turns it.
2. **All three use the language of the market.** Christianity speaks of treasure and profit. The Qur'an speaks of a sale, a trade, a loan and a wage. The Pali speaks of savings, returns, and a field of merit. The difference lies in what is bought, at what price, and from whom.
3. **What the self-interest is redirected toward differs on four counts.**
   - **The end.** It is the person perfected in communion with God (Christianity), a garden of goods with God's pleasure above them (Islam), or the ending of the craving that makes a person (Buddhism).
   - **The price.** In the New Testament entry is not bought at all: "the wages of sin is death; but the gift of God is eternal life" (Rom 6:23). What is bought is reward within it. In the Qur'an the Garden is the price of the believers' "selves and their wealth" (9:111). The Sahihs then say that no deed admits to the Garden without God's mercy (Bukhari 5673), but keep the guarantee to the fighter (Muslim 1876). In the Pali the goal is reached by one's own practice, and the reward of heaven is graded by merit.
   - **Who receives the conduct.** All three direct the means toward other men in giving, honest speech, keeping one's word, and restraint. They part on the enemy. The New Testament commands love of enemies (Matt 5:44). The Qur'an promises the Garden for fighting "in the way of God," grades the fighter above the one who sits (4:95), and makes refusal of affection to kin who oppose God a condition (58:22). The Hadith place "the Garden ... beneath the shades of the swords" (Bukhari 2818). The Twelver texts allow that fighting only under the obligatory Imam. The Pali turns the man away from harm altogether ("one who loves himself should not harm another").
   - **Work in the world.** The New Testament turns the thief into a labourer who gives (Eph 4:28), and the believer is "renewed day by day" (2 Cor 4:16) while working "heartily, as to the Lord" (Col 3:23). The Pali gives the layman a budget, "two parts invest in work" (DN 31), but its highest life leaves the household. The Qur'an names "trade" and "sale" as goods to be held below remembrance of God (24:37), not as something to forsake.
4. **The mechanism of growth.**
   - **Christianity** names progressive sanctification. The believer is among "them that are sanctified" (Heb 10:14), in the Greek "those being sanctified." He is "changed ... from glory to glory" (2 Cor 3:18). The hope of the end drives the present work: "every man that hath this hope in him purifieth himself" (1 John 3:3).
   - **The Pali** has the graduated talk and the four stages, rising from giving to heaven to renunciation. It uses the desire for the goal to end desire (AN 4.159, SN 51.15).
   - **The Qur'an** grades by degrees, but the passages swept name no growth in the person short of the Garden, apart from purification (20:76, 87:14).
5. **What the end does with marriage.**
   - The New Testament: "in the resurrection they neither marry, nor are given in marriage, but are as the angels of God in heaven" (Matt 22:30).
   - The Qur'an and the Hadith give spouses, and for men the Hadith count them, from two to seventy-two (2.15).
   - The Pali end is not a place to which a body goes (MN 72).
6. **The way in is narrow in two of the three.**
   - **Christianity:** entry is a gift by faith, yet holiness is required: "without which no man shall see the Lord" (Heb 12:14).
   - **Islam:** the ways in are many. Each stands under the bridge, the ending and "whom He wills" (2.15).
   - **Buddhism:** each man reaches the goal by his own practice, by degrees, "if you practice partially you succeed partially" (AN 3.87).

## 6. Open items

- **The Sira.**
  - Page numbers are from OpenITI's markers, not checked against print.
  - Ahmad's *Musnad*, al-Hakim and al-Tabari's history were not searched.
  - Guillaume (p. 384) has a line on Quzman that the Saqqa edition lacks.
- **The claim check (2.14).**
  - The reports that give "thinner than the hair and sharper than the sword" to the Prophet are cited through al-Sakhawi, by way of IslamQA. They were not checked in his own text.
  - No fatwa of the Saudi Permanent Committee or of Dar al-Ifta was found on certainty of the Garden or on the bridge.
  - The attribution of the printed *Sharh al-usul al-khamsa* to 'Abd al-Jabbar was not checked.
- **Hadith grades.** Outside Bukhari and Muslim, many of the reports are graded weak. In the Twelver sweep, al-Majlisi grades 292 of the 468 kept *al-Kafi* reports as not sound. Every quotation carries its grade, but the weak reports carry little weight for any argument.
- **The Shia sweep was partial.** *al-Rawda*, volumes 5 to 7 of *al-Kafi*, *Kamil al-ziyarat* and the *Faqih* were swept only for some formulas or chapters. *Faqih* volumes 3 to 5 and al-Majlisi's *Mir'at* itself were not fetched.
- **Gaps in the Muslim edition.** It lacks 2635 and 2971 to 2979, the last including the report of the poor Emigrants entering first, so those reports are not quoted.
- **Other branches.** Ibadi, Zaydi and Isma'ili collections have not been swept.
- **47:4.** The Hafs text reads "were killed"; another canonical reading has "fought." The variant readings were not consulted.
- **2:62 and 5:69 beside 3:85.** The text does not say how these relate, and the note decides nothing.
- **The Pure Abodes.** No passage was found that pairs the non-returner with the Pure Abodes in one sentence. DN 14 was not fetched.
- **Sujato's departures from the literal Pali.** They occur at SN 3.8, Ud 5.1 ("one who desires self-knowledge" for *attakāma*), SN 51.15:4.1 and MN 142's number. Each is glossed above.
- **The SBE wording.** Some of the quotations are from clean OCR, not checked against the page image; they are marked [OCR] in `ext-ref/religion_ends/buddhism.md`.
- **Register for chapter use.** If quoted in a chapter, the King James Version, Müller (1881) and Fausbøll (1881) match the book's register. For the Qur'an, the literal translations here are modern in diction. Rodwell (1861) and Palmer (1880) are the period translations, though less literal.
- **The Catholic and Reformed difference on merit** (Trent VI.16; Westminster Shorter Catechism 33) is noted, not argued.

## 7. Files

All downloads and working files are in `ext-ref/religion_ends/` (git-ignored):

- **Bible.**
  - The KJV is `ext-ref/law_codes/dl/kjv_pg10.txt` (Project Gutenberg 10).
  - The Greek is MorphGNT on the SBLGNT, in `bible/dl/sblgnt/`.
  - The Westminster Shorter Catechism (opc.org) and Trent's Session 6 (Waterworth 1848, Hanover Historical Texts) are in `bible/dl/`.
  - The verse tool is `bible/tools/kjv.py` and the quotation check is `bible/tools/check_quotes.py`. The verse pulls are `bible_*.tsv`.
- **Hadith.**
  - `hadith_bm_conditions.md`, `hadith_bm_descriptions.md`, `hadith_sunan.md` and `hadith_shia.md` hold the master tables, the exclusions and the doubts.
  - `hadith_sira.md` covers the Sira: Ibn Hisham, al-Waqidi and Ibn Sa'd from OpenITI, with Guillaume's pages.
  - `hadith_claim_check.md` covers the claim check. It draws on the creeds, al-Ghazali and the fatwas, all in `hadith/dl/claim/`.
  - The literal translations are `hadith/tools/tr_*.txt`.
  - The texts are in `hadith/dl/`: the hadith-api for the Sunni texts, and thaqalayn and OpenITI for the Shia.
  - `hadith/tools/expand_hadith.py` builds this note's hadith blocks and checks each Arabic excerpt against its source.
- **Qur'an.**
  - `quran_conditions.md` covers the sweep, all categories, bars, exchange, degrees, counts and 21 doubts.
  - `quran_names_descriptions.md` covers names and descriptions.
  - The Tanzil, quran.com, word-by-word and Pickthall downloads are in `quran/dl/`.
  - The literal translations are `quran/tools/tr.txt`. `quran/tools/expand_note.py` builds this note's Qur'an blocks from it and the Tanzil text.
- **Buddhism.**
  - The working file is `buddhism.md`.
  - The bilara-data, SBE X, XXI and XLIX scans, and CBETA T360 and T366 are in `pali/dl/`.
  - The quotation check is `pali/tools/check_note_quotes.py`.
